Quantum Hamiltonian reductions for 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -algebras
Justine Fasquel, Shigenori Nakatsuka

Abstract
. In this paper, we establish a general criterion for good pairs, namely pairs consisting of a nilpotent orbit and an even good grading in a simple Lie algebra, which guarantees the existence of a quantum Hamiltonian reduction between affine 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -algebras. In particular, we show that for type A A , any two affine 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -algebras associated with adjacent nilpotent orbits are related by quantum Hamiltonian reductions.



1.
Introduction

From a finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
over the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, one can construct a vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
, that depends on a complex parameter
\mathsf{k}
. This vertex algebra is called the affine vertex algebra and plays an analogous role to the enveloping algebra of the Kac-Moody algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
. More precisely, its representation theory captures the smooth representations over the affine Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
at level
\mathsf{k}
.

The affine vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
serves as the foundational object from which we can construct a large class of vertex algebras called the
\mathcal{W}
-algebras.
The
\mathcal{W}
-algebra
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
at level
\mathsf{k}
is defined
[
18
,
25
]
by applying to
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
a quantum Hamiltonian reduction corresponding to the nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}\subset\mathfrak{g}
:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})=\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})).

This reduction uses the data of good grading
\Gamma
for a representative nilpotent element
f\in\mathbb{O}
.
Nevertheless, it is known that the resulting
\mathcal{W}
-algebra depends only on the nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
.

\mathcal{W}
-algebras are generally extensions of the Virasoro vertex algebra,
ie.
the symmetry algebra governing two-dimensional conformal symmetry in physics. As such, they play a central role in the study of conformal field theories and in many areas of mathematics connected to them. Originally arising in the context of integrable systems
[
DSJKV
,
DS85
,
18
]
,
\mathcal{W}
-algebras play a fundamental role in the representation theory of affine Kac–Moody algebras
[
1
,
17
,
26
]
, the geometric Langlands program
[
3
,
19
,
20
]
, and more recently the study of our-dimensional supersymmetric gauge theories via 4d/2d duality
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
31
]
and the AGT correspondence
[
BFN16
,
AGT10
,
SV13
]
.

Although each
\mathcal{W}
-algebra
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
is associated with a nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
and is constructed independently as the quantum Hamiltonian reductions (
1.1
), it has been noticed recently that they are indeed related in a way reflecting the closure relations of the nilpotent orbits with respect to the Zariski topology
[
11
,
12
,
15
,
16
,
22
]
.
More precisely, given two nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1},\mathbb{O}_{2}
under the closure relation
{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
, the existence of a (partial or generalised) quantum Hamiltonian reduction
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow{\mathbb{O}}_{1}}
, so that

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow{\mathbb{O}}_{1}}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})\right)\simeq\delta_{n,0}\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})

holds, is expected.
Equation (
1.2
) can be regarded as a vertex-algebraic counterpart of similar known results for Whittaker models for the finite-dimensional Lie algebras (
ie.
finite
\mathcal{W}
-algebras)
[
21
,
29
]
or for the reductive algebraic groups over local fields
[
24
]
.

The finite
\mathcal{W}
-algebras
\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})

[
27
,
30
]
are associative algebras obtained from the enveloping algebras
\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g})
by the quantum Hamiltonian reductions in parallel to the vertex-algebraic case and thus can be viewed as a finite analogue of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
. Indeed,
\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
is also the Zhu algebra of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})

[
5
,
13
]
.
In this finite setting, partial reductions of the form (
1.2
) are better understood.
Indeed, Losev’s decomposition theorem
[
28
]
asserts that the
\hbar
-adic version of
\mathscr{U}(\mathfrak{g})
, say
\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
, is isomorphic to the tensor product of
\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
and a Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{{\mathbb{O}}\,\hbar}
after appropriate completions;
hence a similar statement is naturally expected for
\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})
with the tensor product of
\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})
and some Weyl algebra, namely,

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})^{\wedge}\simeq\mathscr{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})^{\wedge}\ \widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{C}[\![\hbar]\!]}\ \mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\downarrow{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\,\hbar}{}^{\wedge}.

Taking the quantum Hamiltonian reduction yields a finite analogue of (
1.2
).

Main results

In this paper, we consider the
\mathcal{W}
-algebras
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
obtained from representatives
f\in\mathbb{O}
which admit good even gradings (
ie.

\mathbb{Z}
-gradings)
\Gamma
on
\mathfrak{g}
. The gradings are taken to be compatible with the standard triangular decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-}
. For simplicity, we denote
\mathfrak{n}:=\mathfrak{n}^{+}
in the following.
Let
(f_{i},\Gamma_{i})
(
i=1,2
) be two such pairs satisfying the following conditions:

•

Grading condition:
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0,0}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{+}
with respect to the bi-grading
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})
where
\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{+}=\bigoplus_{i,j>0}\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{i,j}
;

•

Nilpotent condition:
f_{\circ}:=f_{2}-f_{1}
lies in
\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{0,-1}
.

Then, one can construct a BRST complex
C_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))
and take the associated quantum Hamiltonian reduction,
ie.
the cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))
.
The main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
, there is an isomorphism of vertex algebras

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))\simeq\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2}).

Moreover, one can also assign a BRST complex
C_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(M)
to any
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})
-modules
M
in a similar way. Thus, we obtain a functor

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\colon\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})\operatorname{-Mod}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})\operatorname{-Mod}.

The isomorphism in Theorem
1.1
is generalized to modules obtained from the Kazhdan–Lusztig category
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
of the affine vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
:

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}\colon\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})\operatorname{-Mod}.

Corollary 1.2
.

For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
M
in
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})
-modules
\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(M)\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}}^{0}(M)
. Hence, the following functors are naturally isomorphic

(1.4)

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}},\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}\colon\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})\operatorname{-Mod}.

Finally, we show that, in type
A
, where we can always find even good gradings, the two conditions mentioned above are satisfied for arbitrary adjacent pairs of nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}
and
\mathbb{O}_{2}
in closure relation
{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\varsubsetneq\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
(denoted
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
).

Theorem 1.3
.

For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
and for arbitrary adjacent nilpotent orbits
{\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}}
in
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, there exists a quantum Hamiltonian reduction
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{1})).

Moreover, (
1.4
) holds.

As a direct consequence, we have the existence of quantum Hamiltonian reductions
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
between all the nilpotent orbits
{\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}}
in closure relation in type
A
. The reduction functor
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
is obtained by composing quantum Hamiltonian reductions appearing in Theorem
1.3
following a “path” of adjacent nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{\alpha_{1}}<\dots<\mathbb{O}_{\alpha_{n}}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
.

Corollary 1.4
.

For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
and for arbitrary nilpotent orbits
{\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}}
in
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, there exists a quantum Hamiltonian reduction
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{1})).

Moreover, we have the following commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{KL}^{k}(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}^{k}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})\operatorname{-Mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}^{k}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})\operatorname{-Mod}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}}}

Organisation of the paper

The rest of the paper is organised as follows.
In §
2
, we introduce the set-up of
the
\mathcal{W}
-algebras.
In §
3
, we recall Wakimoto realisations of
\mathcal{W}
-algebras, which are the main tools to establish the main results.
In §
4
, we present the main results and Theorem
1.1
is proved in §
5
. Finally, in §
6
, we restrict to type
A
and establish Theorem
1.3
.

2.
Preliminary on
\mathcal{W}
-algebras

2.1.
Simple Lie algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional simple Lie algebra over the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-}
a triangular decomposition, and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\right)
the root space decomposition where
\Delta
is the set of roots.
We decompose
\Delta=\Delta^{+}\sqcup\Delta^{-}
into the sets of positive and negative roots, so that
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta^{\pm}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
, and denote by
\Pi=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}\}\subset\Delta^{+}
the set of simple roots.
We fix a set of corresponding (non-zero) root vectors
e_{\alpha}
(
\alpha\in\Delta
) and coroots
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{\ell}\}\subset\mathfrak{h}
so that
C=(\alpha_{j}(h_{i}))_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell}
is the Cartan matrix of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Then
\{e_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Delta\}\cup\{h_{i}\mid i=1,\dots,\ell\}
forms a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and we denote by
c_{\lambda,\mu}^{\nu}
the structure constants:
[h_{i},e_{\alpha}]=c_{i,\alpha}^{\alpha}e_{\alpha}
with
c_{i,\alpha}^{\alpha}=h_{i}(\alpha)
and

[e_{\alpha},e_{\beta}]=\sum_{\gamma\in\Delta}c_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma}e_{\gamma}
.

2.2.
Nilpotent orbits and good gradings

Let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the nilpotent cone,
ie.
the set consisting of elements
f\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathrm{ad}(f)
is nilpotent. It is closed under the adjoint action of the corresponding simple algebraic group
G
of adjoint type whose Lie algebra is
\mathfrak{g}
, which decomposes
\mathcal{N}
into finitely many conjugacy classes, called the nilpotent orbits.
In the case of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, the nilpotent orbits are parametrised by the partitions
\lambda=[\lambda_{1}^{m_{1}},\dots,\lambda_{s}^{m_{s}}]
of
N
— with
\lambda_{1}>\dots>\lambda_{s}>0
and the exponents
m_{i}
denoting the multiplicities — due to the Jordan classification. Setting
\mathbb{O}_{\lambda}
the nilpotent orbit corresponding to the partition
\lambda
,
we have

\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N})=\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in\mathscr{P}_{N}}\mathbb{O}_{\lambda}
with
\mathscr{P}_{N}
the set of partitions of
N
.

Given a nilpotent element
f\in\mathfrak{g}
, a
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\Gamma
on
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{d\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{d}
is said
good
for
f
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(2.1)

\displaystyle f\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1},\quad\operatorname{Ker}\mathrm{ad}(f)\cap\mathfrak{g}_{+}=0,\quad\mathfrak{g}_{-}\subset\operatorname{Im}\mathrm{ad}(f)

where we set
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm}=\bigoplus_{d>0}\mathfrak{g}_{\pm d}
.
The pair
(f,\Gamma)
is then called a
good pair

[
25
]
.
Two good pairs
(f,\Gamma)
,
(f^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})
are equivalent if they are conjugated under the adjoint
G
-action.
Given a (non-zero) nilpotent element, the good gradings are classified up to equivalence
[
14
]
and expressed in terms of pyramids
[
10
]
.

The pyramids are labelled by Young diagrams with shiftings.
We explain concretely with an example.
The Young tableau of the nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}_{[3,2]}
in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{5}
is the diagram

1
2
4
3
5
x
-axis

Here, the boxes are labelled from right to left by the indices
\{1,2,3,4,5\}
of the basis of the natural representation
\mathbb{C}^{5}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{5}
.
From a pyramid, we read the nilpotent element
f
given by

(2.2)

\displaystyle f=\sum_{i\rightarrow j}\mathrm{E}_{i,j}

where the summation is over indexes
(i,j)
such that the boxes

i

and

j

are adjacent with

i

on the left of

j

;
thus the Jordan class of
f
agrees with the nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}_{[3,2]}
we started with.

The grading is obtained by defining a
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-grading on the natural representation by reading the
x
-coordinates for the basis, which correspond to the eigenvalues of the adjoint action of the semisimple element

(2.3)

x=\sum_{i}\left(x_{i}\mathrm{E}_{i,i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}\right)I_{N}

where
x_{i}
is the
x
-coordinate of the centre of the box

i

.
The good gradings defined in this way are compatible with the triangular decomposition in the sense that
\mathfrak{g}_{\pm}\subset\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}
holds. This is the consequence of labelling the pyramids with the smaller numbers to the right.
All the good pairs can be realised in this way up to conjugation by
G
.
We will use the decomposition of the root system
\Delta
with respect to the good grading
\Gamma
:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}=\{\alpha\in\Delta;\Gamma(\alpha)=j\},\quad\Delta_{>0}=\{\alpha\in\Delta;\Gamma(\alpha)>0\}.

In this paper, we only consider
even
good gradings,
ie.
those which are
\mathbb{Z}
-gradings.
In particular, for all nilpotent orbits in type
A
, one can find a good even grading
[
10
,
14
]
.
Therefore, a simple root
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi
has grading
0
or
1
. For convenience, we denote by
I_{j}
(
j=0,1
) the sets of indexes
i=1,\dots,\ell
such that
\Gamma(\alpha_{i})=j
.

2.3.
\mathcal{W}
-algebras

Given a simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, denote by

(2.5)

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{g}[t^{\pm 1}]\oplus\ \mathbb{C}K

the affine Kac–Moody algebra associated with
\mathfrak{g}
.
The universal affine vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
associated with
\mathfrak{g}
at level
\mathsf{k}\in\mathbb{C}
is the parabolic Verma
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
-module

(2.6)

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})=U(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g}[t]\oplus\mathbb{C}K)}\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{k}}

where
\mathbb{C}_{\mathsf{k}}
is the one-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{g}[t]\oplus\mathbb{C}K
on which
\mathfrak{g}[t]
acts trivially and
K
acts as the multiplication by the scalar
\mathsf{k}
.
There is a unique vertex algebra structure on
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
, which is strongly generated by the fields

(2.7)

u(z)=Y(u_{-1},z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{n}z^{-n-1},\qquad u_{n}=u\otimes t^{n},\,u\in\mathfrak{g},

satisfying the OPEs

(2.8)

\displaystyle u(z)v(w)\sim\frac{[u,v](w)}{(z-w)}+\frac{\mathsf{k}(u,v)}{(z-w)^{2}}.

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the normalised invariant bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}
.

In general, we denote by
Y(A,z)
— or briefly
A(z)
—
the field associated with an element
A
in a vertex algebra, by
\mathbbm{1}
the vacuum vector, and use the notation
AB
(instead of
\mathopen{:}AB\mathclose{:}
) for the normally ordered product of
A
and
B
for simplicity.

The
\mathcal{W}
-algebras are vertex algebras obtained as the quantum Hamiltonian reductions of the affine vertex algebras
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})

[
18
,
25
]
.
Fix an
even
good pair
(f,\Gamma)
consisting of a (non-zero) nilpotent element
f
and a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\Gamma
good with respect to
f
.
Let
\bigwedge{}^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\varphi^{*}}
denote the
bc
-system strongly and freely generated by the odd fields
\varphi(z),\varphi^{*}(z)
satisfying the OPEs

(2.9)

\displaystyle\varphi(z)\varphi^{*}(w)\sim\frac{1}{(z-w)},\quad\varphi(z)\varphi(w)\sim 0\sim\varphi^{*}(z)\varphi^{*}(w).

For each positively
\Gamma
-graded root
\alpha
in
\Delta_{>0}
, let us denote by
\bigwedge{}^{\bullet}_{\varphi_{\alpha},\varphi^{*}_{\alpha}}
the copy of
\bigwedge{}^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\varphi^{*}}
generated by the fields
\varphi_{\alpha}(z),\varphi^{*}_{\alpha}(z)
.

The BRST complex associated to the good pair
(f,\Gamma)
is defined as

(2.10)

\displaystyle C_{f}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g}))=\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ch}},\quad\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ch}}:=\bigotimes_{\alpha\in\Delta_{>0}}\bigwedge{}^{\bullet}_{\varphi_{\alpha},\varphi^{*}_{\alpha}}

and equipped with the differential

(2.11)

\displaystyle d=\int Y(Q,z)\ \mathrm{d}z,\quad Q=Q_{\mathrm{st}}+Q_{\chi}

where

(2.12)

Q_{\mathrm{st}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta_{>0}}e_{\alpha}\varphi_{\alpha}^{*}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Delta_{>0}}c_{\alpha,\beta}^{\gamma}\varphi_{\alpha}^{*}\varphi_{\beta}^{*}\varphi_{\gamma},\qquad Q_{\chi}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta_{>0}}(f,e_{\alpha})\varphi_{\alpha}^{*}.

The
\mathcal{W}
-algebra
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},f)
associated with
\mathfrak{g}
and the good pair
(f,\Gamma)
at level
\mathsf{k}
is defined as

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},f)=\mathrm{H}^{0}(C_{f}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})),d).

Note that
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},f)
is freely strongly generated by fields that correspond to a basis of the centraliser
\mathfrak{g}^{f}
homogeneous for the grading
\Gamma

[
26
]
.
However,
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},f)
is independent as a vertex algebra of the choice of the representative
f
in a given nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}
and the good grading
\Gamma

[
4
,
22
]
, so we will occasionally use the notation
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
in the following.
In addition, replacing
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
by a
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M
in (
2.10
), we obtain a functor from the category of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules to the category of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
-modules,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\operatorname{-Mod}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})\operatorname{-Mod}.

For later use, let us rewrite the BRST complex (
2.10
) using the semi-infinite cohomology for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!)
. Consider the one-dimensional
\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!)
-module
\mathbb{C}_{\chi}
on which
\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!)
acts by

(2.15)

u_{n}\mapsto\mathrm{Res}_{t=0}(f,u)t^{n}\mathrm{d}t=\delta_{n,-1}(f,u).

Consider the tensor product
\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!)
-module
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\chi}
and define the semi-infinite complex

(2.16)

\displaystyle C^{\frac{\infty}{2}+\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!);{\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\chi}):=({\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})}\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\chi})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ch}}

equipped with the differential
d_{\mathrm{st}}=\int Y(Q_{\mathrm{st}},z)\mathrm{d}z
, see (
2.11
).
Then, (
2.16
) agrees with the BRST complex
C_{f}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g}))
.
Replacing
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
by a
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-module
M
, we again obtain an isomorphism

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{O}}(M)\simeq\mathrm{H}^{\frac{\infty}{2}+\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{+}(\!(t)\!);M\otimes\mathbb{C}_{\chi}).

3.
Wakimoto realisation

Let
G
be a simple algebraic group (of adjoint type) whose Lie algebra is
\mathrm{Lie}(G)=\mathfrak{g}
.
For simplicity, we denote the positive nilpotent part
\mathfrak{n}^{+}:=\mathfrak{n}
.
Let
N\subset G
be the unipotent subgroup with
\mathrm{Lie}(N)=\mathfrak{n}
.
Using the exponential map
\mathsf{e}:\mathfrak{n}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}N
, one may identify the coordinate rings
\mathbb{C}[N]\simeq\mathbb{C}[z_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Delta^{+}]
where
z_{\alpha}
is the coordinate of the root vector
e_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{n}
(
\alpha\in\Delta^{+}
).
The left multiplication of
N
on itself induces an anti-homomorphism
\rho^{L}:\mathfrak{n}\to\mathcal{D}(N)
where
\mathcal{D}(N)
is the ring of differential operators on
N
.
Then, one has

(3.1)

\rho^{L}(e_{\alpha_{i}})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}P_{i}^{\alpha}(z)\partial_{z_{\alpha}}

for some polynomials
P_{i}^{\alpha}(z)
in
\mathbb{C}[N]
.
More intuitively, by taking a faithful representation of
N
and using the generic element
g(z)=g(z_{\alpha}|\alpha\in\Delta^{+})
, the coefficients
P_{i}^{\alpha}(z)
are uniquely determined by the equality

(3.2)

\mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon e_{i}}g(z)=g(z_{\alpha}+\varepsilon P^{\alpha}_{i}(z)\mid\alpha\in\Delta^{+})

where
\varepsilon
is a dual number,
ie.
a variable satisfying
\varepsilon^{2}=0
.

The affine vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
admits a free fields realisation

(3.3)

\displaystyle\Psi\colon\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Delta^{+}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}

where
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}
denotes the Heisenberg vertex algebra associated with the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
at level
\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Delta^{+}}
is a tensor product of
|\Delta^{+}|
copies of
\beta\gamma
-systems attached to each positive root.
This embedding is known as the
Wakimoto realisation
(see
eg.

[
19
]
).
In addition, it gives rise to a family of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}}:=\mathcal{A}_{N}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}
(
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
) obtained by restriction, called the
Wakimoto representations
. Here
\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}
is the Fock module over
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}
of highest weight
\lambda
.
Note that (
3.3
) is restricted to

(3.4)

\displaystyle\Psi^{R}_{N}\colon\mathcal{V}^{0}(\mathfrak{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Delta^{+}},

which is induced from the
right
multiplication
\rho^{R}\colon\mathfrak{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(N)
.

The affine vertex algebra
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
is isomorphic to the image of
\Psi
. At generic levels, it is equal to the intersection of the kernels of the screening operators

(3.5)

\displaystyle S_{i}=\int S_{i}(z)\ \mathrm{d}z\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{0}}\rightarrow\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{-\alpha_{i}}},\quad S_{i}(z)=Y(\Psi^{L}_{N}(e_{i})\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}{\alpha}_{i}},z),

associated with the simple root vectors
e_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq\ell
).
Here
\Psi^{L}_{N}(e_{i})
is the element in
\mathcal{A}_{\Delta^{+}}
obtained from (
3.1
) by replacing
z_{\alpha}
with
\gamma_{\alpha}
and
\partial_{z_{\alpha}}
with
\beta_{\alpha}
(and taking normally ordered products when necessary).
Moreover, the embedding (
3.3
) is completed into an exact sequence at generic levels
\mathsf{k}
(such as
\mathsf{k}\notin\mathbb{Q}
)

(3.6)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\overset{\Psi}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{0}}\overset{\bigoplus S_{i}}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{-\alpha_{i}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{n_{\circ}}\rightarrow 0

where

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{i}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in W\\
\ell(w)=i\end{subarray}}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{w\circ 0}},

see for instance
[
2
, Proposition 4.2]
.
Here
W
is the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\ell\colon W\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
the length function with
{n_{\circ}}
the length of the longest element, and
\circ\colon W\times\mathfrak{h}^{*}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}^{*}
the dot action given by
w\circ\lambda=w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho
with
\rho
the Weyl vector.

The Wakimoto realisations of the
\mathcal{W}
-algebras are obtained by applying
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}
to (
3.6
).
Since the
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}}
are
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}
-acyclic (see
eg.

[
23
, Proposition 4.5]
), we obtain the complex

(3.8)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{0}})\overset{\bigoplus S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{-\alpha_{i}}})\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathrm{C}_{n_{\circ}})\rightarrow 0,

which is exact by the cohomology vanishing
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{\neq 0}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g}))=0

[
26
,
6
]
.
The
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
-modules
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}})
and the induced screening operators
S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}
are explicitly obtained by using appropriate coordinates systems on
N
.

Setting
\mathfrak{n}_{0}=\mathfrak{n}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, the subalgebra
\mathfrak{n}
decomposes as the semidirect product
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}_{+}
. Correspondingly, we have the group semidirect product
N=N_{0}\ltimes N^{+}
.
Then by
[
23
, Proposition 4.5, Theorem 4.8]
, we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}})\simeq\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O},\lambda}}:=\mathcal{A}_{\Delta^{+}_{0}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}

where
\Delta^{+}_{0}=\Delta^{+}\cap\Delta_{0}
, and the screening operators
S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}
are expressed as

(3.10)

S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}=\int S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}(z)\ \mathrm{d}z,\quad S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}(z):=Y(P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}{\alpha}_{i}},z)

where

(3.11)

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta_{0}}P_{i}^{\alpha}}\beta_{\alpha}&(i\in I_{0}),\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}(f,e_{\alpha})P_{i}^{\alpha}}&(i\in I_{1}).\end{cases}

Roughly, they are obtained from
S_{i}
by evaluating
\beta_{\alpha}
as
\beta_{\alpha}
and
(f,e_{\alpha})
depending on the grading
\Gamma(\alpha)=0,1
.
Since, (
3.2
) leads to

(3.12)

\mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon e_{i}}g=\mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon e_{i}}g_{0}g_{+}=g_{0}\ \mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon(g_{0}\ast e_{i})}g_{+}

where, we set
g\ast u=g^{-1}ug
and use the decomposition
g=g_{0}g_{+}
in
N=N_{0}\ltimes N^{+}
.
Hence, we have more concisely

(3.13)

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}=\Psi^{L}_{N_{0}}(e_{i})\quad(i\in I_{0}),\qquad P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}=(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})\quad(i\in I_{1})

The Wakimoto realisation of the
\mathcal{W}
-algebras is as follows.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
23
]
)
.

At generic levels
\mathsf{k}
, there is an isomorphism of vertex algebras

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})\simeq\bigcap_{i=1,\dots,\ell}\operatorname{Ker}S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\Delta_{0}^{+}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}.

Remark 3.2
.

The Wakimoto realisation still exists for non-generic levels, but the isomorphism in Theorem
3.1
relaxed into an embedding.

Recall that there is an embedding of vertex algebras

(3.15)

\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}^{\natural}}(\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{f})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})

for some level
\mathsf{k}^{\natural}

[
25
]
. The restriction
\mathcal{V}^{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f})\subset\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}^{\natural}}(\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{f})
can be realised explicitly through the Wakimoto realisation as follows.

Proposition 3.3
.

For even good gradings
\Gamma
, the Wakimoto embedding

\Psi^{R}_{N_{0}^{f}}\colon\mathcal{V}^{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Delta_{0}^{+}}

factors through the embedding (
3.14
), ie.

(3.16)

\Psi^{R}_{N_{0}^{f}}\colon\mathcal{V}^{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})\subset\mathcal{A}_{\Delta_{0}^{+}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1
, it suffices to check

[{\hskip 2.84526pt}P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}{\hskip 2.84526pt}{}_{\lambda}{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u){\hskip 2.84526pt}]=0
for
u\in\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}
and all
i
.
For
i\in I_{0}
, the commutativity of two actions (left and right multiplications
N_{0}\curvearrowright N_{0}\curvearrowleft N_{0}
) implies

(3.17)

[{\hskip 2.84526pt}P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}{\hskip 2.84526pt}{}_{\lambda}{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u){\hskip 2.84526pt}]=[{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(e_{i}){\hskip 2.84526pt}{}_{\lambda}{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u){\hskip 2.84526pt}]=0.

For
i\in I_{1}
,
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}=(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})
is expressed only in terms of
\gamma_{\alpha}

(\alpha\in\Delta_{0})
, and thus

(3.18)

[{\hskip 2.84526pt}P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}{\hskip 2.84526pt}{}_{\lambda}{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u){\hskip 2.84526pt}]=-\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u)(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})

where the right-hand side corresponds to the derivation of the function
(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})
by
\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u)
. Hence, it is equal to the
\varepsilon
-linear term, denoted by
\mathrm{CT}_{\varepsilon}(?)
, of
(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})
with replacement
g_{0}\mapsto g_{0}\mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon u}
by definition. Thus

(3.19)

\displaystyle[{\hskip 2.84526pt}P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}}{\hskip 2.84526pt}{}_{\lambda}{\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u){\hskip 2.84526pt}]

\displaystyle=-\rho^{R}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}}(u)(f,g_{0}\ast e_{i})=\mathrm{CT}_{\varepsilon}\left(-(f,(g_{0}\mathsf{e}^{\varepsilon u})\ast e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=(f,[u,g_{0}\ast e_{i}])=([f,u],g_{0}\ast e_{i})=0.

This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Quantum Hamiltonian reductions

Let us consider two good
even
pairs
(f_{i},\Gamma_{i})
(
i=1,2
) and set

(4.1)

\mathfrak{g}_{i,j}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\Gamma_{1}(x)=i\text{ and }\Gamma_{2}(x)=j\},\qquad\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}_{i,j}=\mathfrak{g}_{i,j}\cap\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}

where
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
that define
Assume that the bi-grading
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})

(4.2)

\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{i,j}\supset\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}_{i,j}

and the difference
f_{o}=f_{2}-f_{1}
satisfy the following condition (
\bigstar
):

(
\bigstar
-1)

Grading condition:
\mathfrak{n}(=\mathfrak{n}^{+})
decomposes as

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0,0}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{+},\qquad\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{+}=\bigoplus_{i,j>0}\mathfrak{n}_{i,j};

(
\bigstar
-2)

Nilpotent condition:
f_{o}
lies in
\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{0,-1}
.

Note in particular that the condition (
\bigstar
) implies the inclusion of the nilpotent orbits closures
\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}}_{2}
of
f_{1},f_{2}
, that we denote by
\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq\mathbb{O}_{2}
.
The following properties are clear from the shape of the bi-grading.

Lemma 4.1
.

Under the condition
(\bigstar)
we have the following:

(1)

The subalgebras
\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}
are abelian.

(2)

The bilinear form

(4.4)

\displaystyle\omega:(\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}/\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}})\times\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad(u,v)\mapsto(f_{1},[u,v])

is non-degenerate.

Proof.

For (1), the assertion follows from

(4.5)

[\mathfrak{n}_{1,0},\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}]\subset\mathfrak{n}_{2,0}=0,\qquad[\mathfrak{n}_{0,1},\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}]\subset\mathfrak{n}_{0,2}=0.

For (2), Since
f_{1}=f_{2}-f_{\circ}\in\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{-1,-1}
,
[f_{1},x]
has value in
\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{-1,0}
for
x\in\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}
. Moreover,

(4.6)

[f_{1},\cdot\ ]\colon\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}/\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{-1,0}

is injective.
Now, the assertion follows since
(\cdot,\cdot)\colon\mathfrak{n}^{-}_{-1,0}\times\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is non-degenerate.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The condition presented in
[
22
]
for even gradings corresponds to the case
\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}=0
.

Now, thanks to Proposition
3.3
, one may consider the quantum Hamiltonian reduction by using the following BRST complex:

(4.7)

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))=\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ch}},\quad\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ch}}:=\bigotimes_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}}\bigwedge{}^{\bullet}_{\varphi_{\alpha},\varphi^{*}_{\alpha}}

equipped with the differential

(4.8)

\displaystyle d_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\int Y(Q_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}},z)\ \mathrm{d}z,\quad Q_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}}\left(\alpha+(f_{\circ},\alpha)\right)\varphi_{\alpha}^{*},

where
\mathcal{B}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))
the cohomology.
Note that the shape of the complex and the differential are similar to the usual BRST cohomology defined in §
2.3
, but expressions are simpler than (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) as
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}
and thus
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}
is abelian by Lemma
4.1
.
We note that such a type of differential typically appears for the BRST reduction associated with
the minimal nilpotent orbits in type
A
.
This is the generalisation of the Virasoro and Bershadsky-Polyakov type reductions studied in
[
15
,
16
]
, corresponding to the cases
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2},\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
, respectively.
The main result is the following.

Theorem 4.3
.

For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
, there is an isomorphism of vertex algebras

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))\simeq\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2}).

Let
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
denote the Kazhdan–Lusztig category of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules,
ie.
the category of
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules which are bounded from below by the conformal grading with finite-dimensional graded spaces.
For generic levels
\mathsf{k}
,
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
is semisimple and the simple modules are the Weyl modules

\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}}=U(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U(\mathfrak{g}[t]\oplus\mathbb{C}K)}L_{\lambda},

which are induced from the simple highest weight
\mathfrak{g}
-modules
L_{\lambda}
with dominant integral highest weights
\lambda\in P_{+}
. The isomorphism in Theorem
4.3
is generalised to modules obtained from
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
as follows.

Theorem 4.4
.

For
\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
M
in
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
at generic levels
\mathsf{k}
, there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})
-modules

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}(M)\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}}(M)
.
Moreover, the following functors are naturally isomorphic

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}},\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}\colon\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})\operatorname{-Mod}.

5.
Proof of Theorem
4.3
and
4.4

We decompose the sets of roots
\Delta^{+}
and those of simple roots
I
into

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}=\Delta_{0,0}\sqcup\Delta_{0,1}\sqcup\Delta_{1,0}\sqcup\Delta_{+}^{+},\qquad I=I_{0,0}\sqcup I_{0,1}\sqcup I_{1,0}\sqcup I_{1,1}.

By setting

\Delta_{0}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\Delta_{0,0}\sqcup\Delta_{0,1},\qquad\Delta_{0}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}=\Delta_{0,0}\sqcup\Delta_{1,0},

we obtain the following realisation of
\mathcal{W}
-algebras
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{i})
for
i=1,2
from Theorem
3.1
.

Proposition 5.1
.

At generic levels
\mathsf{k}
, there is an isomorphism of vertex algebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{i})\simeq\bigcap_{i\in I}\operatorname{Ker}S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{i}}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\Delta_{0}^{\mathbb{O}_{i}}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}

with
i=1,2
and

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\begin{cases}\Psi^{L}_{N_{0}}(e_{i}),&\\
\Psi^{L}_{N_{0,1}}(g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&\\
(f_{1},(g_{0}g_{0,1})\ast e_{i}),&\\
(f_{1},g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&\end{cases}\ P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}=\begin{cases}\Psi^{L}_{N_{0}}(e_{i}),&(i\in I_{0,0})\\
(f_{2},(g_{0}g_{1,0})\ast e_{i}),&(i\in I_{0,1})\\
\Psi^{L}_{N_{1,0}}(g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&(i\in I_{1,0})\\
(f_{2},g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&(i\in I_{1,1}).\end{cases}

Proof.

The coefficients
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}
corresponding to the first family of the screening operators are obtained from (
3.2
)
where we write the element
g
in the upper unipotent group
N
as

\displaystyle g=g_{0}g_{0,1}g_{1,0}g_{+},\quad\text{with}\ N=(N_{0,0}\times N_{0,1})\times(N_{1,0}\times N^{+}_{+}),

while the coefficients
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}
are computed using

\displaystyle g=g_{0}g_{1,0}g_{0,1}g_{+},\quad\text{with}\ N=(N_{0,0}\times N_{1,0})\times(N_{0,1}\times N^{+}_{+}),

Both decompositions respect the semidirect product
N=N_{0}\rtimes N^{+}
of §
3
with respect to the grading
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
respectively.

Additionally, note that since
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}
are abelian by Lemma
4.1
(1), the exponential map
\mathsf{e}\colon\mathfrak{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}N
truncates on these subalgebras:

\mathsf{e}\colon\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}\xrightarrow{\simeq}N_{0,1},\quad\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\xrightarrow{\simeq}N_{1,0},\quad u\mapsto\mathsf{e}^{u}=I+u,

simplifying the expression of the coefficients
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}
and
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}
.
∎

Let us derive a more concrete presentation by splitting

\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}=\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}

so that the bilinear form
\omega\colon\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{\omega}\times\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
(see Lemma
4.1
) is non-degenerate.
Take bases
(u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})
,
(u_{n+1},\cdots u_{m})
of
\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{\omega},\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}}
respectively, and a dual base
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
of
\mathfrak{n}_{1,0}
with respect to
\omega
:
\omega(u_{i},v_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
.
Note that by setting
u_{i}^{*}=[v_{i},f_{1}]
and
v_{i}^{*}=[f_{1},u_{i}]
, we have

(u_{i}^{*},u_{j})=\delta_{i,j},\quad(v_{i}^{*},v_{j})=\delta_{i,j}.

Now, let us express
g_{0,1},g_{1,0}
by using these bases:

g_{i,i+1}=e^{A_{i}}=1+A_{i},\qquad A_{0}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}{z_{j}}u_{j},\quad A_{1}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{\widehat{z}_{j}}v_{j}.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have the following:

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\begin{cases}\Psi^{L}_{N_{0}}(e_{i})&\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}(u_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\beta_{j}}&\\
\displaystyle{-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(v_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\gamma_{j}}&\\
(f_{1},g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&\end{cases}\ P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}=\begin{cases}\Psi^{L}_{N_{0}}(e_{i})&(i\in I_{0,0})\\
\displaystyle{(f_{o},g_{0}\ast e_{i})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(u_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\widehat{\gamma}_{j}}&(i\in I_{0,1})\\
\displaystyle{\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(v_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\widehat{\beta}_{j}}&(i\in I_{1,0})\\
(f_{1},g_{0}\ast e_{i}),&(i\in I_{1,1}).\end{cases}

Proof.

The coefficient
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}
, except for
i
in
I_{1,0}
, is immediate from the expression in Proposition
5.1
.
For
i\in I_{1,0}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},(g_{0}e^{A_{0}})\ast e_{i})=\left(f_{1},\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\mathrm{ad}_{A_{0}}^{j}(g_{0}\ast e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=(f_{1},-[A_{0},g_{0}\ast e_{i}])=-([f_{1},A_{0}],g_{0}\ast e_{i})=-\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}(v_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\gamma_{j}

where we used that
\mathrm{ad}_{A_{0}}^{j}(g_{0}\ast e_{i})\in\mathfrak{n}_{1,j}
and
f_{1}\in\mathfrak{n}_{-1,-1}
.

For
i
in
I_{0,0}
and
I_{1,0}
, the computations are clear from Proposition
5.1
again.
The case
i
in
I_{1,1}
follows from the characters
(f_{1},\cdot\ ),(f_{2},\cdot\ )\colon\mathfrak{n}^{+}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
being equal on
\mathfrak{n}_{+}^{+}
.
For
i\in I_{0,1}
, we have

\displaystyle P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}

\displaystyle=(f_{2},(g_{0}e^{A_{1}})\ast e_{i})=\left(f_{2},\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\mathrm{ad}_{A_{1}}^{j}(g_{0}\ast e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=(f_{o},g_{0}\ast e_{i})+([A_{1},f_{1}],g_{0}\ast e_{i})=(f_{o},g_{0}\ast e_{i})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(u_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})\widehat{\gamma}_{j}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{1}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\Delta_{0}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}},\quad u_{i}\mapsto\beta_{i},\quad(n<i\leq m)

by Proposition
3.3
, and thus the differential
d_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}
in (
4.8
) is expressed by

\displaystyle d_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\int Y(Q_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}},z)\mathrm{d}z,\quad Q_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}=\sum_{n<i\leq m}(\beta_{i}+(f_{o},u_{i}))\varphi_{i}^{*}.

Hence, the Poincaré lemma implies the cohomology vanishing for the Wakimoto representations

\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},\lambda}})=\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{N_{0}\times N_{0,1}})\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}\simeq\delta_{\bullet,0}\mathcal{A}_{N_{0}\times N_{0,1}^{\omega}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h},\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}

and thus the resolution (
3.8
) implies the isomorphism of vertex algebras

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{1}))\simeq\bigcap_{i\in I}\operatorname{Ker}[S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]\subset\mathcal{A}_{N_{0}\times N_{0,1}^{\omega}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}

characterized by the induced screening operators

[S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]=\int[S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}](z)\ \mathrm{d}z,\quad[S_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]=[P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}{\alpha}_{i}},

where
[P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]
denotes the cohomology classes of
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}
.
Hence, it suffices to compare
[P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]
and
P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}
for all
i\in I
. It is straightforward to check

(5.1)

\displaystyle[P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]=\begin{cases}P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}&(i\notin I_{0,1})\\
-(f_{o},g_{0}\ast e_{i})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(u_{j}^{*},g_{0}\ast e_{i})&(i\in I_{0,1}).\end{cases}

We then apply the Fourier transform along
N_{0,1}^{\omega}
:

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{N_{0}\times N_{0,1}^{\omega}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{A}_{N_{0}\times N_{1,0}}\otimes\pi_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\mathsf{k}+\mathsf{h}^{\vee}},\qquad\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\mapsto-\widehat{\gamma}_{i},\widehat{\beta}_{i},

which induces an isomorphism

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},\lambda}})\simeq\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{2},\lambda}}

as modules over the free-field algebra corresponding to the case
\lambda=0
. Under this identification, we have

[P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]=\pm P_{i}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}

by comparing (
5.1
) and Lemma
5.2
.
Since the sign factors do not affect the kernels of the associated screening operators, we obtain the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

By
[
2
]
, the Weyl modules
\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}}
admit resolutions by Wakimoto modules, generalising the case
\lambda=0
of (
3.6
):

(5.4)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}}\overset{\bigoplus S_{i,\lambda}}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{s_{i}\circ\lambda}}\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{2}^{\lambda}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{n_{\circ}}^{\lambda}\rightarrow 0

with

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}_{i}^{\lambda}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in W\\
\ell(w)=i\end{subarray}}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{w\circ\lambda}}.

Here,
s_{i}\in W
is the
i
-th simple reflection and
S_{i,\lambda}
are the screening operators given by the formula

(5.5)

\displaystyle S_{i,\lambda}[n]=\int_{\Upsilon}S_{i,\lambda}(z_{1})\dots S_{i,\lambda}(z_{n})\ \mathrm{d}z_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}z_{n}\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda}}\rightarrow\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\lambda-n\alpha_{i}}}

with
n=\lambda(h_{i})+1
for some local system
\Upsilon
on the configuration space
Y_{n}=\{(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})\mid z_{p}\neq z_{q}\}
, see
[
2
]
for details.

Similarly to (
3.8
), we obtain resolutions of the
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O})
-modules
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})

(5.6)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})\rightarrow\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O},\lambda}}\overset{\bigoplus S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}}}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O},s_{i}\circ\lambda}}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}(\mathrm{C}_{n_{\circ}}^{\lambda})\rightarrow 0,

for
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{1},\mathbb{O}_{2}
by applying the functor
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{0}
to (
5.4
). Here, the induced screening operators
S^{\mathbb{O}}_{i,\lambda}
are given by

(5.7)

\displaystyle S^{\mathbb{O}}_{i,\lambda}[n]=\int_{\Upsilon}S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}}(z_{1})\dots S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}}(z_{n})\ \mathrm{d}z_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}z_{n}\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O},\lambda}}\rightarrow\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O},\lambda-n\alpha_{i}}}

by
[
16
, Proposition 8.1]
.
We apply the functor
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
to (
5.6
) for
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}_{1}
and obtain

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}\left(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},\lambda}})&\overset{\bigoplus[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{i=1,\ldots,\ell}\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},s_{i}\circ\lambda}})\\
&\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathrm{C}_{n_{\circ}}^{\lambda}))\rightarrow 0,\end{split}

which gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}\left(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})\right)\simeq\bigcap_{i=1,\dots,\ell}\operatorname{Ker}[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]\subset\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},\lambda}})

of modules over
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}\left(\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g}))\right)\simeq\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})
by Theorem
4.3
.
By using the Fourier transform (
5.2
) and the identification of modules (
5.3
), we obtain
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},\lambda}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{1},s_{i}\circ\lambda}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{2},\lambda}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}^{\mathsf{k}}_{{\mathbb{O}_{2},s_{i}\circ\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]}

for some induced homomorphism in the bottom, which we denote by
[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]
by abuse of notation.
Again, it follows from
[
16
, Proposition 8.1]
that

\displaystyle[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}][n]=\int_{\Upsilon}[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}](z_{1})\dots[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}](z_{n})\ \mathrm{d}z_{1}\dots\mathrm{d}z_{n}

and thus
[S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{1}}]
is identified with
S_{i,\lambda}^{\mathbb{O}_{2}}
by applying the Fourier transform (
5.2
) as in the proof of Theorem
4.3
.
Hence, we obtain isomorphisms

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}}(\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}})

of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathbb{O}_{2})
-modules. Since
\mathrm{KL}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{g})
is semisimple with simple objects
\mathbb{V}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{k}}
, the above isomorphisms immediately give rise to a natural isomorphism of the functors
\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}\circ\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
and
\mathrm{H}^{0}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Adjacent orbits in type
A

Recall that in type
A
,
ie.

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, nilpotent orbits are parametrised by partitions
\mathscr{P}_{N}
of
N
and the partial order on the set of nilpotent orbits is given by the dominance order on
\mathscr{P}_{N}
:
let
\lambda=[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{n}]
and
\mu=[\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n}]
be the partitions of
N
which correspond to the orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}
,
\mathbb{O}_{2}
, respectively,

(6.1)

\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq\mathbb{O}_{2}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq i}\lambda_{j}\leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq i}\mu_{j}\quad(1\leq i\leq n).

Here, we have
\lambda_{i}\geq\lambda_{i+1}
and
\mu_{i}\geq\mu_{i+1}
, but
\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}
might be equal to zero for convenience.
Moreover, two nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
are called
adjacent
if there is no intermediate orbit
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{3}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
.

In this section, we show that any pair of adjacent nilpotent orbits in
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
verifies the condition (
\bigstar
) of §
4
, and thus we obtain the following result by Theorem
4.3
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a generic level.
For two adjacent nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, there exists a quantum Hamiltonian reduction of the minimal type
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{2})\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathbb{O}_{2}\uparrow\mathbb{O}_{1}}^{0}(\mathcal{W}^{\mathsf{k}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{N},\mathbb{O}_{1})).

The rest of this section will be devoted to proving Theorem
6.1
by specifying good pairs satisfying (
\bigstar
). We start with recalling the following well-known result; see for instance
[
Bry
]
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Two nilpotent orbits
\mathbb{O}_{1},\mathbb{O}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
with
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
are adjacent if and only of there exist
1\leq i<j\leq n
such that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=\mu_{i}-1,\quad{\lambda_{i+1}=\lambda_{i+2}=\dots=\lambda_{j}=\mu_{j}+1},\quad\lambda_{k}=\mu_{k}\quad(k\neq i,j)

(6.3)

\displaystyle{\text{and either}\quad j=i+1\quad\text{or}\quad\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i+1}.}

Proof.

We include a proof for reader’s convenience.
We have
\mathbb{O}_{1}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
since (
6.1
) is satisfied. Assume
\mathbb{O}_{1}\leq\mathbb{O}_{3}<\mathbb{O}_{2}
with
\mathbb{O}_{3}
corresponding to the partition
\nu=[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots,\nu_{n}]
.
The dominance order implies directly that
\nu_{k}=\lambda_{k}=\mu_{k}
, for
k<i
. Moreover, since
\nu\neq\mu
and

\begin{gathered}\left(\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\mu_{\ell}\right)-1\leq\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\nu_{\ell}\leq\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\mu_{\ell},\qquad(i\leq k<j)\\
\text{and}\quad\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\nu_{\ell}=\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq k}\mu_{\ell},\qquad(k\geq j).\\
\end{gathered}

there is exactly one of the
i\leq k_{\circ}\!<j
such that
\nu_{k_{\circ}}\!=\mu_{k_{\circ}}\!-1
,
\nu_{k}=\mu_{k}
for
k\neq k_{\circ}
, and
\nu_{k}=\lambda_{k}
for
k\geq j
.

If
j=i+1
, we have
k_{\circ}\!=i
and thus
\nu=\lambda
.
Otherwise, assume
k_{\circ}\!\neq i
, then
\mu_{k_{\circ}}\!=\lambda_{k_{\circ}}
and

\nu_{k_{\circ}}\!=\lambda_{k_{\circ}}\!-1=\lambda_{j}-1=\nu_{j}-1<\nu_{j}

leads to a contradiction. Therefore,
k_{\circ}\!=i
again and
\nu=\lambda
.
∎

The adjacency of nilpotent orbits can be illustrated pictorially on the Young tableaux by moving a box from a row down to a lower row such that all rows in between have the same size. For instance, the next figure illustrates three adjacent nilpotent orbits of
\mathfrak{sl}_{14}
corresponding to, from left to right, partitions
\lambda=[5,3,3,3]
,
\mu=[5,4,3,2]
,
\nu=[6,3,3,2]
— the box in gray is the one we drop at each step:

Remark 6.3
.

The proof of Theorem
6.1
only uses the condition (
6.2
) of Lemma
6.2
. Hence, we also obtain the quantum Hamiltonian reductions between non-adjacent nilpotent orbits satisfying (
6.2
), such that
\lambda=[5,3,3,3]
and
\nu=[6,3,3,2]
in the above example.

Before specifying the representative of
\mathbb{O}_{1}=\mathbb{O}_{\lambda}
and the associated good grading, we prepare some useful lemmas which hold for general even good pairs
(f,\Gamma)
obtained from a Young tableau as in §
2.2
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
(f,\Gamma)
be a good even grading obtained from a Young tableau and written
\mathfrak{n}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathfrak{n}_{d}
the
\Gamma
-grading decomposition.
Then, we have the following

(1)

\mathfrak{n}_{d}=\operatorname{Span}\{\mathrm{E}_{i,j}\mid i<j,\ x(i)=x(j)+d\}
,

(2)

\mathfrak{n}^{f}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}.

Proof.

The first assertion follows directly from the definition of
\Gamma
(see
2.3
) while the second is a consequence of the representation theory of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}=\{e,h,f\}
denote the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple so that

(6.4)

\displaystyle e=\sum_{i\rightarrow j}c_{j,i}\mathrm{E}_{j,i},\quad h{=2x}=\sum_{i\rightarrow j}c_{j,i}(\mathrm{E}_{j,j}-\mathrm{E}_{i,i}),\quad f=\sum_{i\rightarrow j}\mathrm{E}_{i,j}

where
c_{j,i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are some constants, see (
2.2
) for the summation rule.
Thanks to the representation theory of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, we have

\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{f}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cap\{u\in\mathfrak{sl}_{N}\mid\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\cdot u=0\}.

By expressing the partition
\lambda
as
\lambda=[\underline{\lambda}_{1}^{m_{1}},\dots,\underline{\lambda}_{s}^{m_{s}}]
with
\underline{\lambda}_{1}>\dots>\underline{\lambda}_{s}>0
, the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-representation
\mathbb{C}^{N}
, that corresponds to the embedding
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, decomposes as

\mathbb{C}^{N}\simeq\bigoplus_{1\leq a\leq s}(\mathbb{C}^{\underline{\lambda}_{a}})^{m_{a}}

as
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules.
Consider the decomposition

\mathrm{End}_{U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}{(\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\bigoplus_{1\leq a,b\leq s}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{a}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\underline{\lambda}_{a}},\mathbb{C}^{m_{b}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\underline{\lambda}_{b}}),

then Schur’s lemma implies the following:

Lemma 6.5
.

The decomposition
\mathrm{End}_{U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}{(\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\bigoplus_{1\leq a\leq s}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{a}})
induces

\mathfrak{n}^{f}=\mathfrak{n}^{f}_{0}=\left(\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cap\mathrm{End}_{U(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})}(\mathbb{C}^{N})\right)^{f}=\bigoplus_{1\leq a\leq s}(\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cap\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{a}})).

Each summand
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cap\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{a}})
corresponds to the rectangular partition
\lambda_{a}=[\underline{\lambda}_{a}^{m_{a}}]
, and can be written more explicitly as follows:

\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cap\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{m_{a}})=\operatorname{Span}\left\{\widehat{\mathrm{E}}_{i,j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq m_{a}\right\},\qquad\widehat{\mathrm{E}}_{i,j}=\sum_{0\leq k<n}\mathrm{E}_{i+m_{a}k,j+m_{a}k}

by considering the following Young tableau of
\lambda_{a}=[\underline{\lambda}_{a}^{m_{a}}]

m_{a}
\vdots
2
1
2m_{a}
\vdots
m_{a}\!\!+\!2
m_{a}\!\!+\!1
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
N_{a}
\vdots
N_{a}^{\prime}\!+\!2
N_{a}^{\prime}\!+\!1

where
N_{a}^{\prime}=m_{a}(\underline{\lambda}_{a}-1)
and
N_{a}=m_{a}\underline{\lambda}_{a}
.

Proof for Theorem
6.1
.

Thanks to Lemma
6.2
, there exist
1\leq i<j\leq n
such that

(6.5)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=\mu_{i}-1,\quad{\lambda_{i+1}=\dots=\lambda_{j}=\mu_{j}+1},\quad\lambda_{k}=\mu_{k}\quad(k\neq i,j).

We will divide the proof into two steps. We first show the case
(i,j)=(1,n)
(Case I) and then the general case (Case II).

Case I

Assume first
i=1
and
j=n
(
n\geq 2
). Then, we express
\lambda
and
\mu
as follows:

\lambda=[a,\underbrace{b,\dots,b}_{s},b],\qquad\mu=[a+1,\underbrace{b,\dots,b}_{s},b-1].

Note that in the adjacent case,
a=b
when
s\neq 0
but we shall not use this extra condition here.
We take the good gradings associated with the following Young tableaux.
First, for the partition
\lambda
, we associate the left-aligned Young tableau so that the left-end box of each row has the same
x
-coordinate and put a number for each box in the unique way so that

i

sits in the right or below of

j

whenever
i<j
holds.
For the partition
\mu
, we associate the Young tableau obtained from the one for
\lambda
by shifting the boxes on the top by one on the left and dropping the left-end box to the bottom.
For example, for the adjacent partitions
\lambda=[3,3,3]
and
\mu=[4,3,2]
we have

(6.6)

\hbox to49.91pt{\vbox to49.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-8.05133pt\lower-8.45131pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 79.92 11.42 L 57.09 11.42 L 57.09 -11.42 L 79.92 -11.42 Z M 57.09 -11.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.58}{0.0}{0.0}{0.58}{48.05795pt}{-1.86888pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.58}{0.0}{0.0}{0.58}{31.5553pt}{-1.86888pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

In the above examples, the coloured boxes correspond to those shifted in the process of obtaining
\mu
from
\lambda
.

Next, we take the standard representative for the nilpotent element for
\lambda

f_{\lambda}=\sum_{i\underset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}j}\mathrm{E}_{i,j}

as defined in (
2.2
).
Here, we use the notation
i\underset{\lambda}{\rightarrow}j
to emphasize that the summation is done with respect to the Young tableau associated with the partition
\lambda
.
On the other hand, we take a non-standard representative
\widetilde{f}_{\mu}\in\mathbb{O}_{\mu}
obtained as a conjugation of the standard one

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{\mu}=g\ast{f}_{\mu},\qquad{f}_{\mu}=\sum_{i\underset{\mu}{\rightarrow}j}\mathrm{E}_{i,j}

where
g\in\mathrm{SL}_{N}
is an element with
\Gamma_{\mu}
-grading zero which we will specify later.

By Lemma
6.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Span}\{\mathrm{E}_{i,j}\mid i<j,\ x_{\lambda}(i)=x_{\lambda}(j),\ x_{\mu}(i)=x_{\mu}(j)+1\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Span}\{\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{r}+i+j\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r}+(j+1)\mathbf{s}}\mid 0<i<\mathbf{s},\ 0\leq j<b-1\}

where
\mathbf{r}=a-b
and
\mathbf{s}=s+2
. Then it follows from Lemma
6.5
that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{\lambda}}=\operatorname{Span}\left\{\widehat{\mathrm{E}}_{i}\mid 0<i<\mathbf{s}\right\},\quad\widehat{\mathrm{E}}_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{b-1}\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{r}+i+j\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r}+(j+1)\mathbf{s}}

which is indeed an abelian subalgebra. The character used in the quantum Hamiltonian reduction in (
4.8
) is then given by

(f_{\circ},\ \cdot\ )\colon\mathfrak{n}_{0,1}^{f_{\lambda}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad\text{with}\quad f_{\circ}=(\widehat{\mathrm{E}}_{1})^{\mathrm{op}}=\sum_{j=0}^{b-1}\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{r}+(j+1)\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r}+1+j\mathbf{s}}.

We show that
\widetilde{f}_{\mu}:=f_{\lambda}+f_{\circ}
lies in the orbit
\mathbb{O}_{2}=\mathbb{O}_{\mu}
by constructing
g
in (
6.8
).
Let decompose
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
f_{\mu}
row-wise,
ie.

\mathbb{C}^{N}=\mathbb{C}^{a+1}\oplus\underbrace{\mathbb{C}^{b}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathbb{C}^{b}}_{s}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{b-1},\quad f_{\mu}=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{0}+(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{1}+\dots+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s})+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s+1},

where
\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{k}
is given by the sum in (
6.8
) restricted to boxes

i

and

j

on the
(k+1)
-th row.
We have a similar decomposition for
f_{\lambda}=\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{0}+(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s})+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s+1}
and note that

\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s}=\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s}.

It suffices to construct
g
inside
\mathrm{SL}(\mathbb{C}^{a+1}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{b-1})\subset\mathrm{SL}(\mathbb{C}^{N})=\mathrm{SL}_{N}
such that

g^{-1}\ast\left(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{0}+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s+1}+f_{\circ}\right)=\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{0}+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s+1}

since

\displaystyle g^{-1}\ast\widetilde{f}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=g^{-1}\ast\left(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{0}+(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s})+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s+1}+f_{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle=g^{-1}\ast\left(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{0}+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s+1}+f_{\circ}\right)+(\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}^{s})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{0}+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s+1})+(\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{f}_{\mu}^{s})=f_{\mu}.

This reduces to the proof to the case
s=0
of “height two” partitions

\lambda=(a,b),\qquad\mu=(a+1,b-1),

for which the element
g
is constructed by (re-)labelling the boxes of Young tableaux
[
15
,
16
]
:

One can take

\displaystyle g=\mathrm{diag}(\mathfrak{a}I_{\mathbf{r}+1},A_{1},\dots,A_{a-1},a\mathfrak{a}I_{1}),\quad\text{with}\quad A_{i}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(a-i)\mathfrak{b}&i\mathfrak{a}\\
-\mathfrak{b}&\mathfrak{a}\end{array}\right),

where
I_{p}
denotes the identity matrix of size
p
and
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
are constants satisfying
\mathfrak{a}^{a+1}\mathfrak{b}^{b-1}b^{b}=1
,
eg.

(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})=(b^{-1/(a+1)},b^{-1})
.
Hence, the condition
(\bigstar)
holds and the assertion follows as a special case of Theorem
4.3
.

Case II

Let
\lambda,\mu
be general partitions satisfying the condition (
6.5
).
We take the good gradings associated with the Young tableaux as follows. For the partition
\lambda
, we take the Young tableau so that the first
i
rows on the bottom are
right
-aligned,
ie.
the right-end box of each row has the same
x
-coordinate.
Then, we
left
-align the rows
i,\dots,j
, and finally, the rows
j,\dots,n
are
right
-aligned (see the left tableau on (
6.9
) corresponding to the partition
\lambda=(6,5,3,3,3,2)
for an example).
The pyramid is then labelled uniquely so that
i
sits to the right or below of
j
whenever
i<j
holds.
The Young tableau corresponding to the partition
\mu
is obtained from the previous one by shifting the boxes on the top of the pyramid,
ie.
the rows
j,\dots,n
, by one to the left and falling the right-end box of the
j
-th row down. We illustrate this process with the partition
\mu=(6,6,3,3,2,2)
in (
6.9
) below, where the Young tableau on the right is obtained from the left one. In this example,
i=2
and
j=5
.

(6.9)

Note that in the example above, the partitions
\lambda
and
\mu
are again not adjacent. For an adjacent partition
\nu
of
\lambda
, we should have started with a different alignment of rows the Young tableau of
\lambda
and dropped the box from the row
j=5
onto the row
i^{\prime}=3
as illustrated in the following:

(6.10)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

The embeddings of partitions

(6.11)

\lambda^{\circ}=(\lambda_{i},\dots,\lambda_{j})\hookrightarrow\lambda,\qquad\mu^{\circ}=(\mu_{i},\dots,\mu_{j})\hookrightarrow\mu,

straightforwardly induces the embedding of the corresponding Young tableaux.
For example, Young tableaux
in (
6.7
) and (
6.6
) are obtained from those in (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) respectively by removing the dark boxes.
By setting
M=\lambda_{i}+\cdots+\lambda_{j}
, (
6.11
) induce the embeddings

\tau_{\lambda^{\circ}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N},\qquad\tau_{\mu^{\circ}}\colon\mathbb{C}^{M}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}

as
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules, extending the
\mathrm{ad}(f_{\lambda})
-actions given by (
6.4
).
Abusing notation, let also denote by
\tau_{\bullet}\colon\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{M})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N})
the corresponding embeddings by extending trivially.
By
Case I
, we have the existence of
f_{\circ}=\widetilde{f}_{\mu^{\circ}}-f_{\lambda^{\circ}}
and
g
such that
g^{-1}\ast\widetilde{f}_{\mu^{\circ}}=f_{\mu^{\circ}}
.
Setting
\widetilde{f}_{\mu}=f_{\lambda}+\tau_{\lambda^{\circ}}(f_{\circ})
, we find that
(\widetilde{f}_{\mu},\Gamma_{\mu})
is a good pair since
\tau_{\mu^{\circ}}(g^{-1})\ast\widetilde{f}_{\mu}=f_{\mu}
holds. Hence, the condition
(\bigstar)
holds, and the assertion follows as a special case of Theorem
4.3
.
∎
